Facility Location with Public Locations and Private Doubly-Peaked Costs
Richard Cole \insti 1, Pranav Jangir \insti 1

Abstract
In the facility location problem, the task is to place one or more facilities so as to minimize the sum of the agent costs for accessing their nearest facility. Heretofore, in the strategic version, agent locations have been assumed to be private, while their cost measures have been public and identical. For the most part, the cost measure has been the distance to the nearest facility. However, in multiple natural settings, such as placing a firehouse or a school, this modeling does not appear to be a good fit. For it seems natural that the agent locations would be known, but their costs might be private information. In addition, for these types of settings, agents may well want the nearest facility to be at the right distance: near, but not too near. This is captured by the doubly-peaked cost introduced in [ filos2017facility ] . In this paper, we re-examine the facility location problem from this perspective: known agent locations and private preferred distances to the nearest facility. We then give lower and upper bounds on achievable approximations, focusing on the problem in 1D, and in 2D with an L 1 L_{1} distance measure.



Introduction

The strategic version of the facility location problem was formulated in
[
ProcTen2013
]
; the authors noted that it captured decisions about public goods; indeed, where to locate a facility such as a school, but also, for example, issues such as the level of taxation. In these settings there are
n
agents; the task is to locate a facility so that the sum of the distances from the facility to the agents is minimized, where distance is appropriately defined.
Each agent’s goal is to be as close to the facility as possible.
The agents’ positions are private information, creating a need for mechanisms.
The simplest version of the problem has the agents located on the real line.
One simple incentive compatible mechanism is to place the facility at the median of the agents’ locations,
as noted in
[
Moulin1980
]
in the context of voting.

In some applications, for example the placement of a firehouse or school, it is often desirable to be close but not too close to the facility. This version of the problem was introduced in
[
filos2017facility
]
. The authors supposed all agents shared the same preferred distance to the facility. For each agent, this was captured by a cost function of the form
c+\Delta
where
\Delta
was the distance from the facility to the agent’s preferred location for the facility (the reason for the parameter
c
was to enable finite multiplicative approximation factors). Actually, if the agents are located on the real line, each agent would have two preferred locations and
\Delta
would be the distance from the facility to the nearer preferred location. All agents had the same cost function. Again, the private information was the agent locations.

In our view, for these sorts of applications, it seems likely that the agent locations will be public information. For the relevant agents for a school or firehouse are the residents, and presumably the locations of their residences are known. On the other hand, it seems plausible that residents will vary regarding how near or far they want the facility to be.
Accordingly, in this paper, we undertake a systematic study of this version of the problem in which the desired distance to the facility is private information.

Doubly-Peaked Preferences

Double-peaked preferences are an important concept in social choice theory, where they describe situations where preferences are not single-peaked, leading to voting cycles and making majority elections difficult (e.g. see
[
cooter2000strategic
]
). In political economy, voters or political parties might prefer policies that are either aligned with their ideology or highly effective but ideologically distant, rather than moderate policies perceived as ineffective. For instance, as noted in
[
egan2014something
]
, during the Vietnam War, some Americans preferred either complete withdrawal or intensified military action, rather than maintaining the status quo. By incorporating doubly-peaked preferences, economic models can better capture the complex and realistic behavior seen in various social, political, and economic contexts. Apart from locating schools or factories in a neighbourhood, doubly-peaked preferences also capture scenarios where parents might prefer either high public school expenditure (to send their children to good public schools) or low expenditure (to pay less taxes and send children to private schools), rather than moderate spending levels.

These related works highlight that Double-Peaked preferences do have applications in settings more general than literally locating facilities in space. The primary focus of this paper, however, is to study Doubly-Peaked preferences on facility location settings and therefore the modelling assumptions follow the ones normally followed in the facility location literature.

Our Results

Examining the approximation bounds obtained in
[
filos2017facility
]
, we see that they are really additive bounds, where the natural cost function is the distance
\Delta
from the facility to the nearest preferred location. In order to obtain a multiplicative approximation, Filos-Ratsikas et al. used a cost of the form
c+\Delta
, with
c>0
being a suitable parameter.
We prefer to measure the approximation in terms of the additive error, though our bounds could be converted to multiplicative errors in the same way as in Filos-Ratsikas et al., as shown in the appendix.

To distinguish the original version of the problem introduced by Procaccia et al., we call it the private location setting; in contrast, we call the version we are introducing the private cost setting.
We obtain the following results.

•

In 1D, supposing there is a bound
B
on the agents’ preferred distances, any randomized incentive compatible mechanism cannot achieve an approximate solution with expected cost better than
\operatorname{OPT}+\alpha Bn
, where
n
is the number of agents,
\alpha>0
is a constant, and
\operatorname{OPT}
is the cost of an optimal solution. This bound extends to 2D when using
L_{1}
or
L_{2}
distances.

•

For the 1D problem, placing the facility at location
\operatorname{med}
, the median of the agent’s locations, achieves an approximation of
\operatorname{OPT}+nB
. A similar bound,
\operatorname{OPT}+2nB
, holds in 2D when using
L_{1}
or
L_{2}
distances. We call this the Median algorithm.

•

In 1D, we give a deterministic mechanism, called Median-Plus, which always achieves a cost as good as the Median algorithm, and can achieve a
\Theta(nB)
improvement in cost.
We also extend this to a 2D mechanism, 2D-Median-Plus, when using
L_{1}
distances; this extension is a true 2-dimensional computation; it does not reduce to two 1D computations.

•

When the average distances are less than
B
, we obtain better bounds for both Median-Plus and 2D-Median-Plus: If there are
r
agents in the range
\operatorname{med}\pm B
, and the average preferred distance for these agents is
\overline{b}
, then the median algorithm, and hence the Median-Plus mechanisms, achieve a cost of at most
\operatorname{OPT}+2r\overline{b}
.

Related Work

In
[
ProcTen2013
]
, the authors used the facility location problem as a vehicle for studying mechanisms that were approximately optimal in settings without money, such as voting or public facility placement.
For as they showed, strategy-proof optimal mechanisms are often not possible in these settings.
They studied both the social cost version of the problem, introduced above, and a max-cost version,
where the goal is to minimize the maximum of the individual agent’s costs. For the latter problem
they gave a randomized mechanism achieving a multiplicative approximation factor of
3/2
, and showed a lower bound of 2 for deterministic mechanisms. Earlier, in
[
Moulin1980
]
, the author characterized strategy-proof mechanisms in this setting in the context of voting.

Filos-Ratsikas et al.
[
filos2017facility
]
give a randomized mechanism for the private location setting
using the doubly-peaked cost function, achieving cost
\operatorname{OPT}+nB
; here
B
is the (same) preferred distance for all the agents. They also show that in their setting, deterministic mechanisms have
\operatorname{OPT}+\Omega(nB)
cost.

In
[
GolTza2017
]
, the authors argued that measuring the approximation via an additive error rather than a multiplicative factor can be more meaningful in some of these settings. For example, when locating a school, the difference between a 1km and a 2km distance may be much more significant that the difference between 100m and 200m. In
[
CaiFT16
]
, the authors gave an approximate algorithm for minimizing the maximum envy with additive error bounds.

Many other variants of the problem have been studied including limiting the possible locations

[
FeldmanFiat2016
]
; verification (meaning forcing an agent to use the closest declared facility)
[
FotakisTzamos2010
,
nissim2012approximately
]
; public agent locations with private facility preferences
[
fong2018facility
]
;
obnoxious facilities
[
CHENGYZ2013
,
ChengHYZ2019
]
; other geometries including the circle
[
AlonFPT2010
]
and
L_{p}
norms
[
FeigSY2016
]
;
capacitated facilities
[
AzizFLLW2021
]
; reducing variance
[
ProcacciaWajcZhang2018
]
;
\epsilon
-approximate strategy proofness
[
SuiBout2015
,
nissim2012approximately
]
.
The problem of locating two facilities, the 2-facility problem, was also considered in
[
ProcTen2013
]
;
see also
[
LuWZ2009
,
lu2010asymptotically
,
FotakisTzamos2014
,
LuSWZ2010
]
. The
k
-facility problem was studied in
[
FotakisTzamos2016
]
.
An informative recent survey provides a substantially more detailed and thorough overview
[
ChanEtal2021
]
.

Roadmap

We review notation in the next section and explain how to extend a notion of partial group strategy-proofness to our setting. Then, we show the lower bound for the 1D case, followed by the design and analysis of stratetgyproof mechanisms for the 1D case. The remaining results are deferred to the appendix.

Partial Group Strategy Proofness

Lu et al. introduced the notion of
Partial Group Strategy Proofness
in
[
lu2010asymptotically
]
, which is a weaker notion than
Group Strategy Proofness
. This idea was an essential part of their tight
1
1

1

up to constant multiplicative factors.
lower bounds for the two-facility location problem. In our work, we extend the scope of this definition to include doubly peaked preferences.

Definition 1
(For facility location mechanisms with doubly-peaked preferences)

A mechanism is
partial group strategy-proof
if for every set
S
of agents at the same location
\tilde{x}
and having the same distance preference
\tilde{b}
, no subset of agents in
S
can benefit by misreporting.

Formally, let
S\subseteq N
be a group of agents with
x_{i}=\tilde{x}
and
b_{i}=\tilde{b}\leq B
for all
i\in S
. Let
x_{S}=(\tilde{x},\cdots,\tilde{x})\in\Omega^{|S|}
denote the common location of all the agents in this group,
b_{S}=(\tilde{b},\cdots,\tilde{b})\in\Omega^{|S|}
the real preferred distances, and
b^{\prime}_{S}\in\Omega^{|S|}
the potentially misreported (and not necessarily equal) preferred distances.

f
is a partial group strategy-proof mechanism if

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\operatorname{cost}(f((x_{S},b_{S}),(x_{-S},b_{-S})),(\tilde{x},\tilde{b}))\big]

\displaystyle~~~~\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\operatorname{cost}(f((x_{S},b^{\prime}_{S}),(x_{-S},b_{-S})),(\tilde{x},\tilde{b}))\big].

Clearly, partial group strategy-proofness implies strategy-proofness. The following theorem states that strategy-proofness implies partial group strategy-proofness as well. The proof can be found in the appendix.

Lemma 1

In the doubly-peaked preference facility location game, any strategy-proof mechanism is also partial group strategy-proof.

Hardness: Lower bounds on the additive error in ID

We start by analysing the hardness of placing a single facility in the 1D setting when agents have Doubly-Peaked preferences. We show that even when the agents do not have the power to misreport their locations (and can only misreport their preferred distances), the additive approximation error of any strategyproof
f
is at least
\alpha nB
, for a constant
\alpha>0
, which depends on the setting (1D, 2D with
L_{1}
distances, 2D with
L_{2}
distances, universally-truthful mechanism, truthful-in-expectation mechanism, etc.).

In this section, we analyze the hardness for 1D mechanisms. In the 1D case, we prove lower bounds for both deterministic (univerally-truthful) and randomized (truthful-in-expectation) mechanisms. Results for 2D are deferred to the appendix.

Theorem 0.1

Consider an arbitrary deterministic mechanism
f
for the facility location game in 1D with doubly peaked preferences that is universally-truthful. The additive approximation error of
f
is at least
\frac{nB}{24}
. That is, for any given
f
, there exists a worst-case instance
suchthat

SC

(f(,
≥
OPT

( +

nB

24

.\end{theorem}\par\begin{theorem}Consider an arbitrary mechanism $f$ for the facility location game in 1D with doubly peaked preferences that is truthful-in-expectation. The locations are indicated by the circle, diamond and square respectively, and the corresponding preferred locations are indicated by the round brackets, the angle brackets, and the square brackets.}}}\end{figure*}\par\par\par Let$2$beaninstancewhichisidenticalto$1$,exceptthatthelastsetof$m$agents,locatedat$\frac{B}{2}$,haveapreferreddistanceof$\frac{B}{2}$.Formally,let\\
\begin{aligned} \hfil\displaystyle\begin{split}2=\langle(x^{\prime}_{1},b^{\prime}_{1}),...,(x^{\prime}_{m},b^{\prime}_{m}),(x^{\prime}_{m+1},b^{\prime}_{m+1}),\\
...,(x^{\prime}_{2m},b^{\prime}_{2m}),...,(x^{\prime}_{3m},b^{\prime}_{3m})\rangle\end{split}\end{aligned}with\par\begin{aligned} x^{\prime}_{i}=0\text{ and }b^{\prime}_{i}=B&\hskip 7.22743pt\text{for }1\leq i\leq m\\
x^{\prime}_{i}=\tfrac{-B}{4}\text{ and }b^{\prime}_{i}=\tfrac{B}{2}&\hskip 7.22743pt\text{for }m+1\leq i\leq 2m\\
x^{\prime}_{i}=\tfrac{B}{2}\text{ and }b^{\prime}_{i}=\tfrac{B}{2}&\hskip 7.22743pt\text{for }2m+1\leq i\leq 3m.\end{aligned}\par Next,viaaseriesofobservations,weproceedtoprove\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{thm:det-1d-hardness}.\par Let$\mathcal{L}_1 = (-\tfrac{7}{8} B, -\tfrac{5}{8} B)$and$\mathcal{L}_2 = (\tfrac{7}{8} B, \tfrac{25}{24} B)$betwointervals.\par\begin{observation}$\ell^{*}(1)=-\frac{3B}{4}$ and $\operatorname{OPT}(1)=\frac{3mB}{4}$.
\end{observation}\par\begin{proof}To see this note that if the facility is moved away in either direction from location $-\frac{3B}{4}$, at least two agent groups are increasing in cost and at most one is decreasing, until location $0$ is reached. Then, as the facility continues moving to the right, two agent groups have reducing cost and one has increasing cost, until location $\tfrac{B}{4}$ is reached, and then the change in cost switches to at least two increasing and at most one reducing till $\tfrac{B}{2}$ is reached. Finally, as one moves the facility towards $B$, the cost reduces for two agent groups and increases for one agent group. Placing the facility beyond $B$ results in increasing the cost for at least two agent groups.
Calculating the costs at $-\frac{3B}{4}$, $\tfrac{B}{4}$ and $B$ concludes the argument.
\end{proof}\par\begin{observation}For $y\in\mathcal{L}_{1}$, $\operatorname{SC}(y,1)<\frac{3mB}{4}+\frac{mB}{8}=\frac{7mB}{8}$ and for $y\notin\mathcal{L}_{1}$, $\operatorname{SC}(y,1)\geq\frac{7mB}{8}$.
\end{observation}\par\begin{proof}Any point $y\in\mathcal{L}_{1}$ can be written as $y=\frac{-3B}{4}-\delta$ where $-\frac{B}{8}<\delta<\frac{B}{8}$. Thus,
\@@amsalign
Therefore, $\frac{3mB}{4}\leq\operatorname{SC}(y,1)<\frac{3mB}{4}+\frac{mB}{8}=\frac{7mB}{8}$.
\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0ptIf $y\notin\mathcal{L}_{1}$, then $y\in\mathcal{L}_{11}\cup\mathcal{L}_{12}\cup\mathcal{L}_{13}\cup\mathcal{L}_{14}$ where $\mathcal{L}_{11}=(-\infty,-\frac{7B}{8}]\cup[-\frac{5B}{8},-\frac{B}{4}]$, $\mathcal{L}_{12}=(-\frac{B}{4},0]$, $\mathcal{L}_{13}=(0,\frac{B}{2}]$, and $\mathcal{L}_{14}=(\frac{B}{2},\infty)$.
\par For a facility located at $y\in\mathcal{L}_{11}$, the Group 1 agents ($1,\ldots,m$) preferred location is $-B$, the Group 2 agents ($m+1,\ldots,2m$) preferred location is $-\tfrac{3}{4}B$, and the Group 3 agents ($2m+1,\ldots,3m$) preferred location is $-\tfrac{1}{4}B$. Therefore, $\operatorname{SC}(y,1)$ takes its infimum at $-\tfrac{7}{8}B$ and $-\tfrac{5}{8}B$ with $\operatorname{SC}(-\tfrac{7}{8}B,1=\operatorname{SC}(-\tfrac{5}{8}B,1)=\tfrac{7mB}{8}$ and so the cost in $\mathcal{L}_{11}$ is greater than or equal to $\tfrac{7mB}{8}$.
\par For a facility located at $y\in\mathcal{L}_{12}$, the Group 1 agents ($1,\ldots,m$) preferred location is $-B$, the Group 2 agents ($m+1,\ldots,2m$) preferred location is $\tfrac{1}{4}B$, and the Group 3 agents ($2m+1,\ldots,3m$) preferred location is $-\tfrac{1}{4}B$. Therefore, $\operatorname{SC}(y,1)$ is minimized at the median of these three preferred locations, namely at $y=-\tfrac{1}{4}B$, and $\operatorname{SC}(-\tfrac{1}{4}B,1)=\tfrac{5mB}{4}>\tfrac{7mB}{8}$.
\par Similarly, for $y\in\mathcal{L}_{13}$, the preferred locations for the three groups are at $B$, $\tfrac{1}{4}B$ and $-\tfrac{1}{4}B$, respectively. $\operatorname{SC}(y,1)$ is minimized at $y=\tfrac{1}{4}B$ with $\operatorname{SC}(y,1)=\tfrac{5mB}{4}>\tfrac{7mB}{8}$.
\par Finally, for $y\in\mathcal{L}_{14}$, the preferred locations for the three groups are at $B,\tfrac{1}{4}B\text{ and }\tfrac{5}{4}B$, respectively. $\operatorname{SC}(y,1)$ is minimized at $y=B$ with $\operatorname{SC}(y,1)=mB>\tfrac{7mB}{8}$.
\par This proves that $\operatorname{SC}(y,1)<\frac{7mB}{8}$ for $y\in\mathcal{L}_{1}$, and $\operatorname{SC}(y,1)\geq\frac{7mB}{8}$ otherwise.
\end{proof}\par Westatethefollowingobservationswithoutproofastheirproofsaresimilarto\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{obs:det-opt1}and\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{obs:determinstic-interval1}.\par\begin{observation}$\ell^{*}(2)=B$ and $\operatorname{OPT}(2)=\frac{3mB}{4}$.
\end{observation}\par\begin{observation}For $y\in\mathcal{L}_{2}$, $\operatorname{SC}(y,2)<\frac{3mB}{4}+\frac{mB}{8}=\frac{7mB}{8}$ and for $y\notin\mathcal{L}_{2}$, $\operatorname{SC}(y,2)\geq\frac{7mB}{8}$.
\end{observation}\par Wearenowreadytoproveourmaintheorem.\par\begin{proof}Assume for the sake of contradiction that $f$ is a deterministic mechanism such that $\operatorname{SC}(f(,<\operatorname{OPT}(+\tfrac{nB}{24}$ for all instances $.Since$f$isstrategyproof,by\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{thm:grp-strategyproof},$f$mustalsobegroupstrategyproof.\par Considertheinstance$1$(\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq::inst1}).Since$\operatorname{OPT}(1) = \tfrac{3}{4} nB$andsince$f$hasanerroroflessthan$\tfrac{nB}{24}$,by\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{obs:determinstic-interval1},wemusthavethat$f(1) \in\mathcal{L}_1$.\par Similarly,since$\operatorname{OPT}(2) = \tfrac{3}{4} nB$andsince$f$hasanerroroflessthan$\tfrac{nB}{24}$,by\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{obs:determinstic-interval2},wemusthavethat$f(2) \in\mathcal{L}_2$.\par Let$y_1$beanypointin$\mathcal{L}_1$.Clearly,$\operatorname{cost}(y_1, (x_{2m+1}, b_{2m+1})) > -\tfrac{1}{4} B - (-\tfrac{5}{8} B) = \tfrac{3}{8} B$.Let$y_2$beanypointin$\mathcal{L}_2$.Clearly,$\operatorname{cost}(y_2, (x_{2m+1}, b_{2m+1})) < \tfrac{5}{4} B - \tfrac{7}{8} B = \tfrac{3}{8} B$.\par SupposethattheGroup3agentsin$1$misreporttheirpreferreddistancessothattheresultinginstancelookslike$2$.Thismovesthemechanism^{\prime}sfacilitylocationfrom$\mathcal{L}_1$to$\mathcal{L}_2$,reducingtheGroup3agentcosts.Thiscontradictsthefactthat$f$isgroupstrategyproofandthereforeadeterministicstrategyproofmechanism$f$cannothaveanerrorlessthan$\tfrac{nB}{24}$.\end{proof}\par\begin{figure*}\begin{center}\hbox to284.93pt{\vbox to29.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 28.65276pt\lower-20.97604pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{}}

Consider the instances $1$ (\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq::inst1}) and $2$ (\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eq::inst2}) with $n=3m$ agents from \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{thm:det-1d-hardness}. The only difference between the two instances is in the preferred distance of Group 3 agents (the last $m$ agents). We will prove \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{thm:rand-1d-hardness} by contradiction: If there exists a truthful-in-expectation randomized mechanism $f:(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}$ with an additive approximation error smaller than $\tfrac{nB}{300}$, then Group 3 agents in $1$ can reduce their expected cost by misreporting their preferred distances so that the instance looks like $2$.

\par In this section we analyze a simple algorithm that uses only public information, and consequently is trivially strategy proof, the Median algorithm. It simply places the facility at the median of the agent positions
(recall that this is the rank $\lfloor(n+1)/2)\rfloor$ position).
In 2D we consider both the $L_{1}$ and $L_{2}$ metrics. With the $L_{1}$ metric, this means the facility location
has $x$-coordinate equal to the median of the agent's $x$-coordinates, and $y$-coordinate equal to the median of the agents' $y$ coordinates. With the $L_{2}$ metric the median employed is the geometric median,
the location minimizing the sum of the Euclidean distances from the agents.
\par\begin{theorem}In all three settings, on any instance $,theMedianalgorithmhasasocialcostboundedby$\operatorname{OPT}(+2nB$.\end{theorem}\par Ratherthanprovethisbounddirectly,westartwithatighterboundinthecasethattheaveragepreferreddistancesaresmaller.Suppose$r$agentlocationsarewithindistance$B$ofthemedianlocation,andfortheseagentslet$\lx@text@overline{b}$denotetheaverageoftheir$b_i$values.\par\begin{theorem}In all three settings, suppose there are $r$ agents within distance $B$ of the median location,
and suppose the average $b_{i}$ value for these agents is $\overline{b}$.
Then the Median algorithm achieves a cost of at most $\operatorname{OPT}(+2r\overline{b}$.
\end{theorem}\begin{proof}It's helpful to introduce some more notation.
$\operatorname{SC}_{\operatorname{med}}(b)$ denotes the cost of the solution produced by the Median algorithm with preferred distances $b$ (an $n$-vector), and $\operatorname{SC}_{\operatorname{med}}(0)$ denotes the cost of the solution produced by the Median algorithm if all the preferred distances are 0.
Similarly $\operatorname{SC}_{\operatorname{opt}}(b)$ denotes the cost of the optimal solution with preferred distances $b$,
and $\operatorname{SC}_{\operatorname{opt}}(0)$ denotes the cost of this solution with the preferred distances reset to 0.
\par Recall that $\operatorname{med}$ is the facility location chosen by the Median algorithm.
Then,
\@@amsalign
since all agents with $|x_{i}-b_{i}|\geq b_{i}$ reduce their distance to $\operatorname{med}$ by $b_{i}$, when the preferred distance is $b_{i}$ rather than 0; and the other agents increase their distance by at most $b_{i}$.
Similarly,
\@@amsalign
We know that $\operatorname{SC}_{\operatorname{med}}(0)=\operatorname{SC}_{\operatorname{opt}}(0)$, as $\operatorname{med}$ is the optimal solution when the preferred distances are all zero.
Therefore,
\@@amsalign
\end{proof}\par\par\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{thm::med-mech-perf}followsimmediatelyas$\operatorname{OPT}( + 2r\lx@text@overline{b}\leq\operatorname{OPT}( +2n B$.\par AsimplegeneralizationoftheMedianalgorithm,the$k$-Medianalgorithm,achievesthesameapproximationboundforthe$k$-facilityprobleminthe1Dsetting.Thealgorithmsimplyplacesthefacilitiesatthe$k$agentlocationsthatminimizethesumofthedistancesfromtheagentstotheirnearestfacilities(thisplacementisreadilycomputedin$O(n^2 k)$timeusingdynamicprogramming).Theargumentin\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{thm::av-dist-bound}isunchanged.\par\begin{theorem}Suppose $k$ facilities are placed at the agent locations that minimize the social cost if all agents had a preferred distance of 0 (these are the locations provided by the $k$-Median algorithm).
Suppose there are $r$ agents within distance $B$ of the facilities,
and suppose the average $b_{i}$ value for these agents is $\overline{b}$.
Then the $k$-Median algorithm achieves a cost of at most $\operatorname{OPT}(+2r\overline{b}$.
\end{theorem}\begin{proof}It suffices to show that the locations chosen by the $k$-Median algorithm with the preferred 0 distance setting are actually optimal for these distances. To see this, consider the locations in an optimal solution with the preferred 0 distances;
they provide a partitioning of the agents, with each agent assigned to one of its closest facilities. Each partition now forms a 1-facility problem, for which an optimal solution is to place the facility at the median agent's location. Thus there is an optimal solution in which all the facilities are at agent locations, and therefore the minimization performed by the $k$-Median algorithm selects these locations or another set of $k$ locations having this minimum cost.
Applying the argument from \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{thm::av-dist-bound} completes the proof.
\end{proof}Next,wedescribetheMedian-PlusmechanismwhichalwaysachievesacostasgoodastheMedianalgorithm,andcanout-performitbya$\Theta(nB)$factor(asshownintheappendix^{\prime}ssection\emph{ImprovementwithMedian-Plus}).\par\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{section}{toc}{The Median-Plus Mechanism}
\par Throughout this section we use $\mathbb{R}$ instead of $\Omega$ for clarity.
\par\par The optimal solution for Instance $\langle(x_{1},b_{1}),$\ldots$,(x_{n},b_{n})\rangle$ has the following
characteristic. We set $q_{i}=\argmin_{y_{i}=x_{i}\pm b_{i}}\big|\ell^{*}(-y_{i}\big|$, namely Agent $i$'s preferred location that is nearer to the optimal facility location $\ell^{*}($,
breaking ties arbitrarily.
When $n$ is odd, $\ell^{*}($ is the median of $\{q_{1},q_{2},\ldots,q_{n}\}$, and when $n$ is even, there is a tie-breaking
for which $\ell^{*}($ is a median of the resulting locations.
\par As our mechanism does not know $\ell^{*}($, for each Agent $i$ it selects $i$'s preferred location $p_{i}$ using a rule based on the position of the agent compared to the median agent position. Then the mechanism takes the median of the preferred locations as the facility location.
We will show this mechanism achieves at least as good an approximation to the optimal solution as the median mechanism described in the previous section.
\par\begin{algorithm}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{1}}{Median-Plus Mechanism for one facility in $\mathbb{R}$}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{\bf Mechanism\lx@nobreakspace 1{}}}{Median-Plus Mechanism for one facility in $\mathbb{R}$}}}
\lx@prepend@indentation@{}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline{\hbox{\hbox{{{Input:}} }}$\langle(x_{1},b_{1}),$\ldots$,(x_{n},b_{n})\rangle$.\lx@prepend@indentation@{}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline}{\hbox{\hbox{{{Output:}} }}The facility location\lx@prepend@indentation@{}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline}\lx@prepend@indentation@{}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline\algocf@printnl@i{{{2}}}Let $\operatorname{med}$ be the median of $\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}$, the rank $m=\lfloor(n+1)/2\rfloor$ agent position.\lx@prepend@indentation@{}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline{{\algocf@printnl@i{{{3}}}for}}\ {\emph{each agent $i$}}\ {{do}}\lx@prepend@indentation@{}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline\algocf@printnl@i{{{4}}}(* set $p_{i}$, $i$'s preferred facility location *) \lx@prepend@indentation@{\enskip\lx@algo@rule\quad}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline{{\algocf@printnl@i{{{7}}}if}}\ {\emph{$x_{i}\leq\operatorname{med}$}}\ {{then}}\lx@prepend@indentation@{\enskip\lx@algo@rule\quad}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline\algocf@printnl@i{{{8}}}$p_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}+b_{i}$
\lx@prepend@indentation@{\enskip\lx@algo@rule\quad\enskip\lx@algo@rule\quad}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline{{\algocf@printnl@i{{{9}}}else}}\lx@prepend@indentation@{\enskip\lx@algo@rule\quad}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline\algocf@printnl@i{{{10}}}$p_{i}\leftarrow x_{i}-b_{i}$
\lx@prepend@indentation@{\enskip\lx@algo@rule\quad\enskip\lx@algo@rule\quad}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline{}\lx@prepend@indentation@{\algocf@printnl@i{{{11}}}\enskip\lx@algo@rule\quad}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline{}\algocf@printnl@i{{{12}}}Output: the median (rank $m=\lfloor(n+1)/2\rfloor$) of the locations $\{p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{n}\}$.
\end{algorithm}\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\par For the following lemmas and theorems, we assume that agents in $\langle(x_{1},b_{1}),$\ldots$,(x_{n},b_{n})\rangle$ are sorted in increasing order by their locations $x_{i}$. This doesn't affect the optimum facility location and clearly, doesn't affect our mechanism.

\end{subfigure}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{3}}{Two cases when $x_{i}\geq\operatorname{med}$. The red star represents $f($---the output of Median-Plus when agents report truthfully. The orange bracket is agent $i$'s apparent preferred facility location when she misreports $b^{\prime}_{i}<b_{i}$. The green bracket shows the location when she misreports $b^{\prime}_{i}>b_{i}$.}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}\par\begin{lemma}Mechanism Median-Plus is strategy proof.
\end{lemma}\par\begin{proof}Since the positions of the agents are fixed, $\operatorname{med}$ is fixed and therefore for every agent, $p_{i}$ is either $x_{i}-b_{i}$ or $x_{i}+b_{i}$ depending on its position.
\par Let $f($ denote the output of Median-Plus on $.Let$ = \langle(x_1, b_1) \ldots(x_i, b'_i) \ldots(x_n, b_n) \rangle$betheinstancewhenagent$i$misreportstheirpreferreddistanceas$b'_i \leq B$.Wewillshowthat\begin{equation*}\operatorname{cost}(f(,(x_{i},b_{i}))\leq\operatorname{cost}(f(),(x_{i},b_{i})).\end{equation*}\par Withoutlossofgenerality,assume$x_i > \operatorname{med}$,whichimplies$p_i = x_i - b_i$.Therearetwocases(asshownin\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig::1d-strategyproof}).\\
\noindent{\sc Case1}(\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig::1d-strategyproof-case1}):$f( \leq p_i$.If$b'_i < b_i$(orangebracket),$p'_i = x_i - b'_i > p_i$and$f() = f($.If$b'_i > b_i$(greenbracket),$p'_i = x_i - b'_i < p_i$;thus$f() \leq f($andtherefore$\operatorname{cost}(f(, (x_i, b_i)) \leq\operatorname{cost}(f(), (x_i, b_i))$.\\
\noindent\newline
{\sc Case2}(\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig::1d-strategyproof-case2}):$f( > p_i$.If$b'_i > b_i$(greenbracket),then$p'_i = x_i - b'_i < p_i$and$f() = f($.If$b'_i < b_i$(orangebracket),then$p'_i = x_i - b'_i > p_i$.Observethatsince$b'_i \geq 0$,if$x_i \leq f($,then$p'_i \leq x_i$impliesthat$f() = f($.Otherwise,if$x_i > f($,then$f() \in[f(, x_i]$andtherefore,$\operatorname{cost}(f(, (x_i, b_i)) \leq\operatorname{cost}(f(), (x_i, b_i))$.\end{proof}\par Next,weshowthatMedian-PlusalwaysachievesatleastasgoodacostasMedian.Let$SC_{\operatorname{medp}}($denotethecostachievedbyMedian-Pluson$, and $\operatorname{SC}_{\operatorname{med}}($ the cost achieved by the Median algorithm.
\begin{lemma}On any instance $,$\operatorname{SC}_{\operatorname{medp}}(\leq\operatorname{SC}_{\operatorname{med}}($.\end{lemma}\begin{proof}Consider the $n$ preferred locations used by Mechanism Median-Plus.
Let $y_{\operatorname{medp}}$ be the facility location it chooses.
We pair the locations with one location on either side of $y_{\operatorname{medp}}$
(one of these locations is $y_{\operatorname{medp}}$, and in the event that $n$ is even, one of the other locations
is paired with $y_{\operatorname{medp}}$. Let $p$ and $q$ be paired locations.
Then the contribution of $p$ and $q$ to $\operatorname{SC}_{\operatorname{medp}}($ is $|p-q|$, while their contribution to
$\operatorname{SC}_{\operatorname{med}}($ is at least $|p-q|$. If $n$ is odd, the unpaired location $y_{\operatorname{medp}}$ contributes 0 to $\operatorname{SC}_{\operatorname{medp}}($ and at least 0 to $\operatorname{SC}_{\operatorname{med}}($. Summing over all paired locations yields the claim.
\end{proof}\par Thefollowingboundisimmediatefrom\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{thm::av-dist-bound}.\par\begin{corollary}Suppose that in instance $thereare$r$agentlocationsintherange$\operatorname{med}\pm B$,andsupposetheiraverage$b_i$valueis$\lx@text@overline{b}$.ThentheMedian-Plusmechanismachievesacostofatmost$\operatorname{OPT}(+2r\lx@text@overline{b}$.\end{corollary}\par ThisalsoimpliesthatMedian-Plusalwaysachievesacostofatmost$\operatorname{OPT}(+2nB$.Intheappendix,weshowhowtoimprovethisboundto$\operatorname{OPT}(+nB$(see\lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lem::basic-mech-perf}).\newline
\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\noindent Intheappendix,wegeneralizetheMedian-Plusmechanismtohandlesettingswithagentslocatedin2D,whenusing$L_1$distancesasthecostmetric.\par\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{section}{toc}{Improvement with Median-Plus}
\par In Lemmas \ref{lem::med-plus-better-than-med} and \ref{lem::2d-med-plus-better-than-med} we prove that Median-Plus and 2D-Median-Plus perform at least as well as the median algorithm. In this section we demonstrate how the potential improvements to the social cost can be $\Theta(nB)$ on \emph{strongly skewed instances}.
\par In a skewed instance all agents on one side of the median (say $x_{i}<\operatorname{med}$) have preferred distances such that both the preferred locations of the agents lie on the same side of the median as the agent location (if $x_{i}<\operatorname{med}$, then $x_{i}+b_{i}<\operatorname{med}$; for the instance to be strongly skewed we require $x_{i}+b_{i}<\operatorname{med}-\Theta(B)$). On the other hand, agents on the other side of the median (say $x_{i}>\operatorname{med}$), have their two preferred distances on different side of the median (if $x_{i}>\operatorname{med}$, then $x_{i}-b_{i}<\operatorname{med}$; for the instance to be strongly skewed we require $x_{i}+b_{i}<\operatorname{med}-\Theta(B)$).
Consider a skewed instance in 1D, as shown in \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig::skewed-instance}.
\par\begin{figure}\centering\par\par\par\par\hbox to173.38pt{\vbox to33.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-127.035pt\lower-157.20859pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.75pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{4}}{Skewed Instance. Median and one of its preferred location is marked with black color. Agents to the left of median are colored orange along with their preferred locations and agents to the right of median are colored purple. The output of Median-Plus $\operatorname{medp}$ is marked in a red star.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 4}}{\small Skewed Instance. Median and one of its preferred location is marked with black color. Agents to the left of median are colored orange along with their preferred locations and agents to the right of median are colored purple. The output of Median-Plus $\operatorname{medp}$ is marked in a red star.}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}\par If the instance is strongly skewed, it can be readily observed that moving the median from $\operatorname{medp}$ to $\operatorname{med}$ increases the social cost by $\Theta(nB)$, when all the $b_{i}=\Theta(B)$.
\par Note that an instance need not be \emph{perfectly strongly skewed} for $\operatorname{medp}$ to be a strictly better solution.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{section}{toc}{Conclusion}
\par We have introduced and investigated a new version of the facility location problem with public locations and private costs.
We showed a $\Theta(nB)$ lower bound on the additive approximation factor, gave a simple algorithm which uses just public information to match this bound up to constant factors, and developed a non-trivial mechanism that matches and can substantially outperform this mechanism, both for the 1D and 2D with $L_{1}$ costs versions.
\par As we indicated, an analog of the median algorithm solves the $k$-facility problem, and again achieves a $\Theta(nB)$ additive approximation faction. This leaves open the question of whether there are any strategy-proof mechanisms which leverage the bids to improve on this performance.

\par\par\par\begin{proof}Observe that $\operatorname{cost}(y,(x,b))$ can be written as
\begin{equation}\operatorname{cost}(y,(x,b))=\lvert d(x,y)-b\rvert\end{equation}where $d(x,y)$ denotes the distance between $x$ and $y$. If $x,y\in\mathbb{R}$, then $d(x,y)=|x-y|$ and if $x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}$, then $d(x,y)$ can be the $L_{1}$ distance or the $L_{2}$ distance between the points. \\
\noindent{\sc Case 1}: $d(x,y)\leq\min(b,b^{\prime})$. In this case, by \eqref{eq::triangle-ineq-dist}, $\operatorname{cost}(y,(x,b))-\operatorname{cost}(y,(x,b^{\prime}))=b-b^{\prime}\leq|b-b^{\prime}|$. \\
\noindent\newline
{\sc Case 2}: $d(x,y)\geq\max(b,b^{\prime})$. Similar to Case 1, here $\operatorname{cost}(y,(x,b))-\operatorname{cost}(y,(x,b^{\prime}))=b^{\prime}-b\leq|b-b^{\prime}|$. \\
\noindent\newline
{\sc Case 3}: $\min(b,b^{\prime})<d(x,y)<\max(b,b^{\prime})$. If $b<b^{\prime}$, then $\operatorname{cost}(y,(x,b))-\operatorname{cost}(y,(x,b^{\prime}))=2d(x,y)-b-b^{\prime}<b^{\prime}-b=|b^{\prime}-b|$. Similarly, if $b>b^{\prime}$, $\operatorname{cost}(y,(x,b))-\operatorname{cost}(y,(x,b^{\prime}))=b+b^{\prime}-2d(x,y)<b-b^{\prime}=|b^{\prime}-b|$.
\end{proof}\par\begin{figure*}\begin{center}\hbox to284.93pt{\vbox to29.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 28.65276pt\lower-20.97604pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{}}

$\tfrac{B}{4}$ and $B$, respectively.
For the second claim, note that $\operatorname{cost}(y,(x^{\prime}_{1},b^{\prime}_{1}))+\operatorname{cost}(y,(x^{\prime}_{m+1},b^{\prime}_{m+1}))$ achieves the minimum value of $\frac{B}{4}$ when $y\in[-B,\frac{-3}{4}B]$. Therefore, when $y\notin\mathcal{L}_{2}$, $\operatorname{cost}(y,(x^{\prime}_{1},b^{\prime}_{1}))+\operatorname{cost}(y,(x^{\prime}_{m+1},b^{\prime}_{m+1}))\geq\frac{B}{4}$.
\end{proof}\par\par\begin{proof}Let $p=\mathbb{P}\big[y\in\mathcal{L}_{2}~|~y\sim f(2)\big]$. Assume, for a contradiction, that $p<\frac{19}{20}$. \\
\par Observe that $\mathbb{E}_{y\sim f(2)}[\operatorname{SC}(y,2)]=$
\@@amsalign
\par\par By \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{obs:opt2}, $\mathcal{O}_{2}=\frac{3mB}{4}$. Therefore, $\mathbb{E}_{y\sim f(2)}\big[\operatorname{SC}(y,2)~|~y\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\big]\geq\frac{3mB}{4}$. By \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{obs:interval2}, $\mathbb{E}_{y\sim f(2)}\big[\operatorname{SC}(y,2)~|~y\notin\mathcal{L}_{2}\big]\geq\frac{19mB}{20}$. Substituting these inequalities in equation \eqref{eq:prob2} gives
\@@amsalign
\par However, this is a contradiction because, by assumption, $\mathbb{E}_{y\sim f(2)}\big[\operatorname{SC}(y,2)\big]<\mathcal{O}_{2}+\frac{mB}{100}=\frac{76mB}{100}=\frac{19mB}{25}$.
\par Therefore, $p=\mathbb{P}\big[y\in\mathcal{L}_{1}~|~y\sim f(1)\big]\geq\frac{19}{20}$.
\end{proof}\par\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Lower Bounds for 2D}
\par\par\par\par\par\par\begin{theorem}Consider an arbitrary mechanism $f$ for the facility location game in 2D with the $L_{1}$ distance measure and with doubly peaked preferences that is truthful-in-expectation. The additive approximation error of $f$ is at least $\frac{nB}{500}$.

\par\par\par\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{(b)}}{Modified $2$. $\mathcal{R}_{2}$ is shaded in gray for $\beta=\tfrac{4}{5}$. Point X is $(\tfrac{4B}{5},0)$, Y is $(\tfrac{7B}{8},\tfrac{B}{8})$ and Z is $(\tfrac{16}{15}B,0)$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{\small(b)}}{\small Modified $2$. $\mathcal{R}_{2}$ is shaded in gray for $\beta=\tfrac{4}{5}$. Point X is $(\tfrac{4B}{5},0)$, Y is $(\tfrac{7B}{8},\tfrac{B}{8})$ and Z is $(\tfrac{16}{15}B,0)$.}}}
\end{subfigure}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{6}}{An extended construction of the 1D setting. Agent group $1,2$ and $3$ marked by black points. New groups of $\beta m$ agents marked by red points at $(-\tfrac{3}{4}B,0)$ and $(B,0)$. Note that in the two instances, the value of $\beta$ is different so that the regions are defined as stated in the proof of \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{thm:2d-hardness}. In the final construction we use $\beta=1$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 6}}{\small An extended construction of the 1D setting. Agent group $1,2$ and $3$ marked by black points. New groups of $\beta m$ agents marked by red points at $(-\tfrac{3}{4}B,0)$ and $(B,0)$. Note that in the two instances, the value of $\beta$ is different so that the regions are defined as stated in the proof of \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{thm:2d-hardness}. In the final construction we use $\beta=1$.}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}\par\begin{proof}We will be using the same construction as in the 1D setting with the same groups of agents arranged in the same locations on a line. We will introduce a further two groups, each of $\beta m$ agents, at locations $-\tfrac{3}{4}B$ and $B$, resp., with each new agent having its $b_{i}=0$. This increases both optimal social costs $\mathcal{O}_{1}$ in $1$
and $\mathcal{O}_{2}$ in $2$ to $\tfrac{3}{4}Bm+\tfrac{7}{4}\beta Bm$.
\par The goal is to identify regions $\mathcal{R}_{1}$ and $\mathcal{R}_{2}$ which will play the same roles as $\mathcal{L}_{1}$ and $\mathcal{L}_{2}$ did in the proof of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{thm:rand-1d-hardness}.
We then reprove Observations \ref{obs:interval1}--\ref{obs:gps1-2} (with the values of $\mathcal{O}_{1}$ and $\mathcal{O}_{2}$ appropriately updated). Lemmas \ref{lemma:prob1}--\ref{lemma:dist1} now follow with unchanged proofs, while the proof of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lemma:dist2} needs a minor change to account for the new agents; the concluding proof of the theorem is also unchanged. This yields an error lower bound of $\frac{Bm}{100}$, as in \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{thm:rand-1d-hardness}.
All that changes is the value of $n$, which is now $3m+2\beta m$.
\par Specifically, we need the following to hold:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@As in \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{obs:interval1}, for
$y\in\mathcal{R}_{1}$, $\operatorname{SC}(y,1)\leq\mathcal{O}_{1}+\frac{4mB}{20}$ and for $y\notin\mathcal{R}_{1}$, $\operatorname{SC}(y,2)\geq\mathcal{O}_{1}+\frac{4mB}{20}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@As in \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{obs:interval2}, for
$y\in\mathcal{R}_{2}$, $\operatorname{SC}(y,2)\leq\mathcal{O}_{2}+\frac{4mB}{20}$, and for $y\notin\mathcal{R}_{2}$, $\operatorname{SC}(y,2)\geq\mathcal{O}_{2}+\frac{4mB}{20}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@As in \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{obs:gp3}, for Group 3 agents, their cost in $\mathcal{R}_{1}$ is at least $\frac{3B}{10}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@As in \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{obs:gps1-2}, for all $y\in\mathcal{R}_{2}$,
$\operatorname{cost}(y,(x^{\prime}_{1},b^{\prime}_{1}))+\operatorname{cost}(y,(x^{\prime}_{m+1},b^{\prime}_{m+1}))\geq\tfrac{3B}{4}$ and
for $y\notin\mathcal{R}_{2}$,
$\operatorname{cost}(y,(x^{\prime}_{1},b^{\prime}_{1}))+\operatorname{cost}(y,(x^{\prime}_{m+1},b^{\prime}_{m+1}))\geq\tfrac{B}{4}$.
\end{itemize}\par\par Next, we specify the regions $\mathcal{R}_{1}$ and $\mathcal{R}_{2}$. We only describe these regions for $y\geq 0$ as the regions for $y<0$ are symmetric (see \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig::2d-hardness}).
$\mathcal{R}_{1}$ will comprise a triangle
with base $\mathcal{L}_{1}$ and apex at height $\overline{h}$ on the left boundary of the Group 2 agent diamonds (see \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig::2d-hardness-inst1}), where $\overline{h}$ is a value we will specify shortly.
\par Now we show the analog of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{obs:interval1}, as specified above.
We want that the cost of the boundary $\mathcal{R}_{1}$ be exactly $\mathcal{O}_{1}+\tfrac{mB}{5}$.
The cost at the triangle apex is $\mathcal{O}_{1}+2mB\beta\cdot\overline{h}$, as long as $\overline{h}\leq\tfrac{B}{4}$
(the height of the point at which the Group 2 and Group 3 diamonds intersect).
Thus it suffices to have $\beta\overline{h}=\tfrac{B}{10}$.
As we will see when we define $\mathcal{R}_{2}$, we will want $\beta=\tfrac{2}{5}$, and therefore
$\overline{h}=\tfrac{B}{4}$.
By inspection, the cost strictly inside the boundary of $\mathcal{R}_{1}$ is always less that $\mathcal{O}_{1}+\tfrac{mB}{5}$.
This can be seen by considering the change in costs as one moves horizontally from the boundary.
Similarly, the cost outside of $\mathcal{R}_{1}$ is always strictly greater than $\mathcal{O}_{1}+\tfrac{mB}{5}$.
Again, this can be verified by considering the change in cost due to horizontal moves away from this boundary
(this is somewhat more painstaking).
\par\par To verify the analog of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{obs:gp3}, we note that in region $\mathcal{R}_{1}$, at height $h$, Group 3 agents will have cost at least
$(-\tfrac{B}{4}+h)-(\tfrac{-3B}{4}+h)-(\tfrac{B}{5}-2\beta\cdot h)\geq\tfrac{3}{10}B$, as desired.
\par We turn to $2$ and $\mathcal{R}_{2}$. $\mathcal{R}_{2}$ will comprise a triangle with base $\mathcal{L}_{2}$ and apex at height $\tilde{h}$ on the right boundary of the Group 1 agent diamonds (see \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig::2d-hardness-inst2}).
$\tilde{h}$ will be chosen so that the cost on the boundary is exactly $\mathcal{O}_{2}+\tfrac{mB}{5}$.
At the triangle apex, the cost is $\mathcal{O}_{2}+2mB\beta\cdot\tilde{h}$, so long as $\tilde{h}\leq\tfrac{B}{8}$
(this is the height of the intersection of the diamonds for Groups 2 and 3).
We choose $\tilde{h}=\tfrac{B}{8}$, and therefore $\beta=\tfrac{2}{5}$.
We note that the point $(\tfrac{B}{8},\tfrac{B}{8})$ (the previously mentioned intersection) also has cost
$\mathcal{O}_{2}+2mB\beta\tilde{h}=\mathcal{O}_{2}+\tfrac{mb}{5}$, so we add it to $\mathcal{R}_{2}$.
Again, by inspection, all points strictly inside $\mathcal{R}_{2}$ have cost less than $\mathcal{O}_{2}+\tfrac{mb}{5}$,
and all points outside this region have strictly larger cost.
\par Finally, the analog of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{obs:gps1-2} is really verified by inspection: in $\mathcal{R}_{2}$, in combination, a Group 1 and a Group 2 agent have cost at least $\tfrac{3}{4}B$,
and outside this region, they have cost at least $\tfrac{1}{4}B$.
\par However, the proof of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lemma:dist2} needs a few changes (basically, to add in and then cancel the contribution of the new agents to the social cost). We now have
\@@amsalign
yielding
\@@amsalign
\par As in the proof of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lemma:dist2}, using \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{obs:gps1-2}, we obtain
\@@amsalign
\par We also observe that $\mathbb{E}\big[\text{new agent costs}\big]\geq\tfrac{7}{4}mB\beta$.
\par Substituting these two bounds into \eqref{eqn:new-lem10-cost-bdd} gives
\@@amsalign
On dividing by $m$ this proves \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lemma:dist2}.
\par To conclude the proof, we note that the approximation factor is at least $\tfrac{1}{100}mB$. And $n=3m+2\beta m=3.8m$. This yields an approximation factor of $\frac{1}{380}nB$. (Strictly speaking, we need $m\beta$ to be an integer, as this is the number of new agents at each of the new agent locations; it suffices to have $m$ be an integer multiple of 5.)
\end{proof}\par\par\par\par\begin{theorem}Consider an arbitrary mechanism $f$ for the facility location game in 2D with the $L_{2}$ distance measure and with doubly peaked preferences that is truthful-in-expectation. The additive approximation error of $f$ is at least $\frac{nB}{630,500}$.
\end{theorem}\begin{proof}As in the proof of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{thm:2d-hardness} we introduce an additional $\beta m$ agents at each of the locations $(\tfrac{-3B}{4},0)$ and $(B,0)$. The shape of regions $\mathcal{R}_{1}$ and $\mathcal{R}_{2}$ will change a bit, but we will achieve the same bounds on the costs that are needed to deduce that the approximation factor must be at least $\frac{mB}{100}$.
To this end, we define $\mathcal{R}_{1}$ to comprise those points in $1$ with social cost at most $\mathcal{O}_{1}+\tfrac{mB}{5}$, and $\mathcal{R}_{2}$ to comprise those points in $2$ with social cost at most $\mathcal{O}_{2}+\tfrac{mB}{5}$.
In addition, we need that
for each Group 3 agent, the cost inside $\mathcal{R}_{1}$ be at least $\frac{3B}{10}$. Similarly, in $2$, the social cost outside $\mathcal{R}_{2}$ is at least $\mathcal{O}_{2}+\frac{mB}{5}$,
and
we will need to modify \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{obs:gps1-2} to account for the new agents, again causing minor changes to the proof of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lemma:dist2}. The modified \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{obs:gps1-2} states that excluding Group 3 agents, the cost
inside $\mathcal{R}_{2}$ is at least $\tfrac{3mB}{4}+\tfrac{7mB\beta}{4}$ and outside $\mathcal{R}_{2}$ is at least $\tfrac{mB}{4}+\tfrac{7mB\beta}{4}$.
\par This leads to the following modification of the proof of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lemma:dist2}. Letting $p$ denote the probability that the mechanism chooses a facility location in $\mathcal{R}_{2}$, we bound
\@@amsalign
using the fact that $p\geq\tfrac{19}{20}$, as in the proof of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lemma:dist2}.
\par Now, as in the proof of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{thm:2d-hardness}, we substitute this bound into \eqref{eqn:new-lem10-cost-bdd}, which yields the bound stated in \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{lemma:dist2}.
\par The additional cost due to the new agents at a location $(Bx,By)$ with $-1\leq x\leq 1$ and $y\geq 0$
is
\@@amsalign
We first ensure that when $By>\tfrac{B}{60}$, the contribution of this term is at least $\mathcal{O}_{1}+\tfrac{Bm}{5}$, so these values of $By$ lie outside both $\mathcal{R}_{1}$ and $\mathcal{R}_{2}$. This function is minimized when $1-x=\tfrac{3}{4}+x$, i.e.\ $x=\tfrac{1}{8}$, so it suffices to have $\beta(\tfrac{49}{64}+\tfrac{1}{3600})\geq\tfrac{3}{4}+\tfrac{7\beta}{4}+\tfrac{1}{5}$; $\beta\geq 3151$ suffices.
\par We turn to the analysis of $1$. First, we observe that if $Bx\leq\tfrac{-11}{20}B$, then
each Group 3 agent has cost at least $\tfrac{3}{10}B$, so for any location in this range in $\mathcal{R}_{1}$,
the Group 3 agents have the desired cost. We conclude the analysis of $1$ by showing that all locations with $Bx>\tfrac{-11}{20}B$ have cost greater than $\mathcal{O}_{1}+\tfrac{4}{20}mB$ and hence lie outside
$\mathcal{R}_{1}$.
\par We begin by noting that if the social cost due to the agents in Groups 1--3 at location $(Bx,0)$ is at least $mB$, then the social cost at location $(Bx,By)$ for $0\leq y\leq 1/60$ is at least $\tfrac{19}{20}mB$, as the cost for each agent in Groups 1--3 decreases by at most $B/60$, and the cost for the new agents only increases. Therefore only values of $x$ we still need to consider are $\tfrac{-11}{20}<x<\tfrac{-1}{2}$.
The social cost in this range is given by the following expression (with $mB$ factored out to reduce clutter).
\@@amsalign
We perform a power series expansion to allow us to lower bound this expression.
\@@amsalign
We use the bounds $y\leq 1/60$, $-11/20\leq x\leq-1/2$, to absorb the $y^{4}$ terms into the $y^{2}$ terms, as follows.
\@@amsalign
if $\beta\geq 2$.
\par This demonstrates that we achieve the required bounds in $1$.
\par Next, we analyze $2$. Now the only values of $x$ of concern are $x>\tfrac{3}{4}B$ (because the points $(x,0)$ with $x>\tfrac{3}{4}B$ are the only ones with Groups 1--3 contributing social cost less than $Bm$). Note that we are only interested in those points in $\mathcal{R}_{2}$, whichever they are. So it is enough to lower bound the Group 1 plus Group 2 cost plus new agent cost by $(\tfrac{3}{4}+\frac{7}{4}\beta)mB$ in the range $x>\tfrac{3}{4}B$.
We carry this out via two cases: $x^{2}+y^{2}\geq 1$ and $x^{2}+y^{2}<1$, again using a power series in $x^{2}/y^{2}$.
\par\par{\sc Case 1}: $x^{2}+y^{2}<1$.\\
The Group 1, Group 2 and new agents contribute the following cost to the social cost.
\@@amsalign
We use the bounds $y\leq 1/60$, $\tfrac{3}{4}\leq x\leq 1$, yielding:
\@@amsalign
if $\beta\geq 1$.
\par\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\noindent{\sc Case 2}: $x^{2}+y^{2}\geq 1$.\\
We break this case into two different cases: \\
\noindent{\sc Case 2a: $\tfrac{3}{4}\leq x\leq 1$}. \\
The Group 1, Group 2 and new agents contribute the following cost to the social cost.
\@@amsalign
We use the bounds $x^{2}+y^{2}\geq 1$, $y\leq 1/60$, $\tfrac{3}{4}\leq x\leq 1$. Let $x=1-\delta$ for $\delta<\tfrac{1}{4}$. Since $x^{2}+y^{2}\geq 1$, we have $y^{2}\geq\delta(2-\delta)$. Substituting these values yields:
\@@amsalign
Note that $\frac{2\beta\delta(2-\delta)}{7}-\frac{\beta\delta(2-\delta)}{27\cdot 60^{2}}-\delta$
\@@amsalign
if $\beta>4$ (since $\delta<\tfrac{1}{4}$).
\par So $\operatorname{SC}(x,y)\geq\frac{3}{4}+\frac{7}{4}\beta$, as desired.
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent{\sc Case 2b: $x>1$}. \\
Now
\@@amsalign
as desired.
\par\par Finally, by inspection, the Group 1 plus Group 2 costs are always at least $\tfrac{mB}{4}$ and the new agents always contribute at least $\tfrac{7}{4}mB\beta$, verifying the remaining part of the analog of \lx@cref{creftypecap~refnum}{obs:gps1-2}.
\par Since $\beta=3151$, we conclude that $n=6305m$, and as the additional cost in the approximation is $100Bm$,
this yields the claimed lower bound.
\end{proof}\par We note that the constant in the last theorem could be improved substantially, but at the cost
of having many more cases to analyze.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{subsection}{toc}{2D Median Plus}
\par In this section, we generalize the Median-Plus mechanism to handle settings with agents located in 2D,
when using $L_{1}$ distances as the cost metric. Throughout this section $\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{2}$, and the facility location instance is $\langle((x_{1},y_{1}),b_{1}),\ldots,((x_{n},b_{n}),b_{n})\rangle$.
\par Placing the facility at the median of agent's $x$ and $y$ coordinates gives an approximation within $2nB$ of the optimal cost $\operatorname{OPT}$. In this section, we design a mechanism that performs at least as well as the Median algorithm for every instance $,andpotentiallyimprovesthecostby$\Theta(nB)$byutilizingagent^{\prime}spreferreddistances.Webeginbydefiningsomenotationwewillbeusing.\par Let$\operatorname{med}= (\operatorname{med}_x, \operatorname{med}_y)$denotethecoordinate-wisemedianoftheagent^{\prime}slocations.$\operatorname{med}_x$isthemedianofthepoints$(x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_n)$,and$\operatorname{med}_y$isthemedianofthepoints$(y_1, y_2, \ldots, y_n)$.Theoptimalfacilitylocationforanagent$i$liesonadiamondatadistance$b_i$from$(x_i, y_i)$,asshownin\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig::vertical-lines}.\par\begin{figure}\centering\begin{subfigure}[155.24895pt]\centering\par\par\par\par\par\hbox to112.08pt{\vbox to188.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-164.625pt\lower-195.9pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.75pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{(b)}}{Vertical Split Line constructed from the agent's v-EHDs.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{\small(b)}}{\small Vertical Split Line constructed from the agent's v-EHDs.}}}
\end{subfigure}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{7}}{Agent X, Y and Z with their preferred locations. Locations where an agent has zero cost form a diamond centered at their location. The red star marks the coordinate-wise median of the agent locations.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 7}}{\small Agent X, Y and Z with their preferred locations. Locations where an agent has zero cost form a diamond centered at their location. The red star marks the coordinate-wise median of the agent locations.}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}\par\begin{definition}Let Agent $i$ be located at $(x_{i},y_{i})$ and have a preferred distance $b_{i}$. We define two zig-zag lines, named $L$ and $R$. Each consists of half the boundary of a diamond at distance $b_{i}$ from $(x_{i},y_{i})$, plus vertical extensions to $y=\pm\infty$. More precisely:
\@@amsalign
If $x_{i}\leq\operatorname{med}_{x}$, then v-EHD for agent $i$ is defined as the set of points in $R$ and otherwise it is defined as the points in $L$. For an instance $,v-EHDforanagent$i$canalsobeinterpretedasafunction$v_i$ofthe$y$coordinatesuchthatthepointsoftheform$(v_i(y), y)$definethev-EHDofagent$i$(\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig::vertical-ehd}).\end{definition}\par\begin{definition}Let $v_{i}$ denote the v-EHD of agent $i$. For $y\in\mathbb{R}$, let $\vec{v}(y)=(v_{1}(y),\ldots,v_{n}(y))$. The Vertical split line is defined by the function $\mathcal{V}(y)=\operatorname{median}(\vec{v}_{y})$ (see \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig::vertical-splitline}).
\end{definition}\par TheHorizontalExtendedHalfDiamonds(h-EHDs)andHorizontalSplitLinearedefinedanalogously,andillustratedinFigures\ref{fig::horizontal-splitline}and\ref{fig::splitline-intersection}.\par\par\begin{figure}\centering\begin{subfigure}[155.24895pt]\centering\par\hbox to136.2pt{\vbox to156.79pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-149.7pt\lower-186.12498pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\par\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{9}}{Vertical (yellow) and Horizontal (blue) split lines intersecting on a line segment (red) and separating thereafter. The regions that bound the two split lines are shaded. Note that the regions that bound the split lines are disjoint after the two lines get separated.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 9}}{\small Vertical (yellow) and Horizontal (blue) split lines intersecting on a line segment (red) and separating thereafter. The regions that bound the two split lines are shaded. Note that the regions that bound the split lines are disjoint after the two lines get separated.}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}\par\begin{proof}(Sketch.)~
In order to see that there can only be one intersection (at a single point or a line segment), we traverse through the split lines simultaneously. Starting from the uppermost point of intersection, we traverse the vertical split line from top to bottom and traverse the horizontal split line as follows: \begin{itemize}\itemize@item@\emph{left to right} if the intersection is at a single point or if it's on an edge that is going to the right and down. (see \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig::split-line-intersection-proof})
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@\emph{right to left} if the intersection edge is going to the left and down.
\end{itemize}\par Now, without loss of generality, assume that the intersection is either a single point or an edge that is going to the right and down.
Note that from any given point on the horizontal split line, its continuation must lie in a region
bounded by two lines, one heading up and right at 45\lx@text@degree and one heading down and right at 45\lx@text@degree(\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig::split-line-intersection-proof}).
Similarly the continuation of the vertical split line is bounded by two lines, one heading down and right at 45\lx@text@degree and one heading down at left at 45\lx@text@degree(\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig::split-line-intersection-proof}). This implies that once the two split lines intersect they can only continue their intersection along a single line heading down and to the right.
And once they separate, the regions for their continuation will become disjoint (\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig::split-line-intersection-proof}). Consequently, they intersect either at a single point or along a single edge.
\par We also note that this single edge is formed by a single diamond. For example, if we consider the vertical split line, if two diamonds share an edge, when the corresponding v-EHDs separate, the left-to-right order is maintained (modulo tie-breaking along the shared edge).
\par\end{proof}\par\par\begin{figure}\par\par\par\hbox to136.95pt{\vbox to188.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-149.325pt\lower-196.275pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.75pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\par{}{{}}{}

\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{10}}{Intersection of vertical and horizonal split lines. In this instance, the intersection is a line segment on agent X's diamond and is highlighted in red.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{\small Figure 10}}{\small Intersection of vertical and horizonal split lines. In this instance, the intersection is a line segment on agent X's diamond and is highlighted in red.}}}
\end{figure}\par Withthehelpofthesedefinitions,wedesignthefollowingmechanismthatusesagents^{\prime}preferreddistancestoimproveovertheMedianalgorithm.\par\begin{algorithm}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{2}}{2D-Median-Plus mechanism for one facility in $\mathbb{R}^{2}$ with $L_{1}$ distance metric}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{\bf Mechanism\lx@nobreakspace 2{}}}{2D-Median-Plus mechanism for one facility in $\mathbb{R}^{2}$ with $L_{1}$ distance metric}}}
\lx@prepend@indentation@{}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline{\hbox{\hbox{{{Input:}} }}$\langle((x_{1},y_{1}),b_{1}),$\ldots$,((x_{n},y_{n}),b_{n})\rangle$.\lx@prepend@indentation@{}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline}\lx@prepend@indentation@{}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline\algocf@printnl@i{{{2}}}Compute the horizontal and vertical split lines.
\lx@prepend@indentation@{}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline{\hbox{\hbox{{{Output:}} }}$\operatorname{med}^{+}$, the intersection point of the vertical and horizontal split line. If there are multiple such points, return the lowest cost point, and if there are several, output the one nearest to $\operatorname{med}$.\lx@prepend@indentation@{}\lx@algo@@endline\lx@algo@@startline}\end{algorithm}\par\par\par\begin{theorem}2D-Median-Plus is strategyproof.
\end{theorem}\begin{proof}The key observation is that the only agents who can affect the location $\operatorname{med}^{+}$ computed by the mechanism are those for which at least one of their reported v-EHD or h-EHD include the location $\operatorname{med}^{+}$.
Otherwise a small change to an agent's declarations does not affect the median of the v-EHDs and h-EHDs at $\operatorname{med}^{+}$, and so this remains the location reported by the mechanism.
\par A little more care is needed to justify this claim in the event that the intersection of the two split lines is an edge rather than a point. But even then, a small change to an agent's declarations does not affect the relative costs on this edge because changing its $b_{i}$ declaration has the same effect on all the points on the edge, as the edge on the intersection is either completely outside the agent's declared diamond or completely inside, and so the location $\operatorname{med}^{+}$ is still unchanged.
\par Now if the change is sufficient to touch the location $\operatorname{med}^{+}$, then further changes of the declared preferred distance could move the location $\operatorname{med}^{+}$. First, let's suppose the correct declaration has $\operatorname{med}^{+}$ outside Agent $i$'s diamond. We will describe Agent $i$'s bid as if she is continuously increasing her bid to bring her diamond to the actual $\operatorname{med}^{+}$ and then beyond it; at first, this increase could drag the computed $\operatorname{med}^{+}$ with the expanding diamond; eventually, the computed $\operatorname{med}^{+}$ moves no further and as the diamond boundary is beyond the computed $\operatorname{med}^{+}$, further expansion has no effect.
But note that the expansion only moves the computed $\operatorname{med}^{+}$ further from Agent $i$'s preferred locations, namely the diamond boundary with the truthful declaration.
\par A similar argument applies if $\operatorname{med}^{+}$ lies inside Agent's $i$'s ``correct'' diamond. Here a shrinking diamond may drag the computed $\operatorname{med}^{+}$ away from the boundary of its correct diamond. So in both cases, Agent $i$ has no incentive to make an incorrect declaration.
\end{proof}\par\begin{lemma}The social cost of 2D-Median-Plus is at least as good as the social cost of the Median algorithm for every instance $.\end{lemma}\begin{proof}Let $\operatorname{med}=(\operatorname{med}_{x},\operatorname{med}_{y})$ denote the median location, and let $\operatorname{med}^{+}=(\operatorname{med}^{+}_{x},\operatorname{med}^{+}_{y})$ denote the location returned by the 2D-Median-Plus Mechanism.
To facilitate the remaining discussion, WLOG suppose that $\operatorname{med}$ is above and to the left of $\operatorname{med}^{+}$.
We imagine continuously moving the facility from location $\operatorname{med}$ to location $\operatorname{med}^{+}$ in such a way that the social cost is always non-increasing. To this end, the overall move is made in a series of alternating horizontal and vertical moves,
always moving away from $\operatorname{med}$ and toward $\operatorname{med}^{+}$. The moves are further constrained as follows. Each horizontal move stops no later than the vertical split line (i.e.\ it does not cross this split line) and each vertical move stops no later than the horizontal split line.
Note that a horizontal move could cross the horizontal split line, and similarly for a vertical move.
\par\par There is possibly one additional final move along a diagonal edge. In the event that the two split lines intersect in an edge, this edge could have a uniform cost, or could have increasing cost in one direction or the other.
As $\operatorname{med}^{+}$ is the low-cost endpoint, the move along this edge will either reduce the cost or leave it unchanged.
\par Now we justify the claim regarding the social cost being non-increasing. Consider Agent $i$'s cost.
Let $d_{i}$ be the distance from the facility to Agent $i$ and let $b_{i}$ be Agent $i$'s preferred distance.
Then, if the facility is outside Agent $i$'s diamond, its cost is $d_{i}-b_{i}$, and if inside its cost is $b_{i}-d_{i}$. Suppose the facility moves distance $\delta$ horizontally. If the facility is outside $i$'s diamond and the move is away from the diamond, then $d_{i}$ increases by $\delta$. If the facility is inside, and the move is away from the nearest boundary, then $d_{i}$ decreases by $\delta$. In both cases, the cost increases by $\delta$. With moves in the opposite directions, the cost decreases by $\delta$.
\par The next observation is that a horizontal move is toward at least $\lfloor(n+1)/2\rfloor$ v-EHDs and away from at most $\lceil(n-1)/2\rceil$ (these two numbers could be equal if $n$ is even). Furthermore, as we will show shortly, the toward moves are all cost decreasing. Note that if this claim is true then the social cost must be non-increasing.
\par Clearly, if the facility is on the outside of the diamond defining a v-EHD, and the move is toward the v-EHD, then the cost is decreasing. If inside the diamond, the issue is whether the facility is moving toward or away from the agent: moving away reduces the cost, while moving toward would increase it.
Consider an R-type v-EHD. This can occur only when the agent is aligned with or to the left of $\operatorname{med}$, and as the facility is aligned with or to the right of location $\operatorname{med}$ and moving to the right in a horizontal move,
it must be moving away from the agent, i.e.\ cost reducing.
Now consider an L-type v-EHD. In this case the facility is moving away from the v-EHD; so this v-EHD is on the minority side of $\operatorname{med}$. While the cost may increase for this v-EHD, as the movement is toward the side with the majority of v-EHDs, overall the movement does not increase the cost.
\par An analogous argument applies to the vertical moves.
\par\end{proof}\par\par\par\end{document}
